Like everything suddenly seemed possible and I was completely sure things were about to be amazing, even if nothing had really changed enough to explain why I felt that way.\n\nAnd it wasn’t just being in a good mood for a day or two. It felt bigger than that, like I had this energy or excitement running through me. But at the same time, I could get really touchy. My husband would make some small comment about the budget, or something around the house, and I’d just snap at him like he had said something terrible. Then later I’d think, why did I react that strongly?\n\nSo yes, there have been periods like that, and they lasted more than several days. More like weeks, at least the ones I’m thinking of.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks when you felt lit up, did you need less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I’m trying to think about those weeks specifically, and I don’t have a clean memory of, like, “Oh yes, I was only sleeping three hours and felt fine,” or anything that clear.\n\nI do remember feeling more awake or keyed up in general during those times, but whether I actually needed less sleep than usual… I don’t know. I might have stayed up later some nights because I felt like my mind was still going, but I can’t confidently say my sleep was dramatically reduced or that I didn’t feel tired afterward.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is: maybe a little, but I didn’t track it, and it’s not one of the things I remember clearly.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks when you felt lit up, were you more driven than usual to start or do a lot of projects or activities?\n\nPatient: Yes. That part I remember pretty clearly, actually. I would suddenly feel like I had this whole plan for my life and I needed to act on all of it immediately.\n\nLike, I started contacting galleries, and I was drafting sections for a newsletter, and then I was planning these community workshop ideas, and at the same time trying to line up freelance clients. It wasn’t just one project where I was excited and focused — it was like five different directions at once, and they all felt urgent and totally realistic in the moment.\n\nLooking back, it was way more than I could actually follow through on. I’d start making lists and sending emails and sketching out plans, and I’d feel like, “Okay, this is it, I’m finally getting everything moving.” But then there was just too much happening, and a lot of it didn’t really get finished or went nowhere because I had taken on more than I could manage. So yes, definitely more driven than usual.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks when you felt lit up, did you feel more confident or powerful than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes, I did. I don’t think I would have called it “powerful” at the time, because that sounds so dramatic, but… I definitely felt unusually sure of myself. Like I had finally figured out something everyone else hadn’t, or like I was suddenly capable of making these huge things happen if I just pushed hard enough.\n\nI remember honestly believing I could take the newsletter idea, get a gallery partnership going, and turn that into some kind of consulting business or bigger professional thing almost overnight. And I mean, looking back, I hadn’t really worked out the basics. I didn’t have a clear business plan, or enough contacts, or even a realistic sense of how all of it would actually function. But at the time it didn’t feel unrealistic. It felt obvious, like, “Of course I can do this. Why wouldn’t this work?”\n\nIt wasn’t just normal confidence, I guess. It was more like this certainty that I was on the edge of something big and that I could pull it all together faster than anyone would expect. I felt kind of unstoppable in that way, even though now I can see there were a lot of missing pieces.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks when you felt lit up, did that cause serious problems in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it catastrophic, but it definitely caused problems. More than I wanted to admit at the time.\n\nThe biggest thing is that I couldn’t actually keep everything organized. I’d have all these plans and ideas and I’d be starting things, but the normal stuff in my life started slipping. I fell behind in my certificate coursework because I kept thinking I was going to catch up later, or I’d sit down to do it and get pulled into some other plan that felt more urgent. And then suddenly I was behind enough that it felt embarrassing to even look at it.\n\nAt home, things piled up too. Bills, meals, basic household tasks — not because I didn’t care, but because I was just not following through consistently. I’d have these bursts where I thought I was going to reorganize everything, and then somehow the actual practical things didn’t get done. My spouse had to step in more, and I feel bad about that because it wasn’t fair. It put more on them.\n\nAnd socially, I kind of pulled back from people and from art commitments I normally really care about. Which is strange, because I felt like I was doing all these big important things, but in reality I was dropping the regular things and relationships that keep me steady.\n\nSo yes, it did cause problems. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. It wasn’t just a day or two where I was in a good mood or had a burst of motivation. It lasted for weeks at a time, that kind of lit-up feeling where everything seemed exciting and possible and like it was all about to turn into something amazing.

I remember being really sure of myself during those stretches, like things were finally clicking and I just had to keep moving with it. But it wasn’t only upbeat, exactly. I could get irritable too, especially at home. If my husband made a small comment about the budget or the house, things that were probably reasonable to bring up, I’d snap at him like he was trying to slow me down or criticize me. At the time it felt like he was being negative, but looking back, my reaction was bigger than the comment deserved.

So yes, definitely at least a full week. More like several weeks, from what I remember.

I mean, “depressed” feels like a big word, and I don’t know if what I’m feeling counts as that or if it’s more just being worn out and kind of weighed down. I wouldn’t say I’m walking around crying all the time or anything like that, but I also don’t feel like myself exactly.\n\nIt’s more like… everything has this dullness to it, I guess. Like I’m trying to do the normal things I should be able to do, and they just feel harder than they should. So maybe that is feeling down? I don’t know. I’m not trying to dodge the question, I just don’t really have a clean answer.\n\nDoctor: After physical or mental activity, do you feel significantly worse afterward?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. It’s not always dramatic like I collapse afterward, but even pretty normal things can take more out of me than they should. Like if I unload the dishwasher or try to sit and read for my certificate class, afterward I can feel like I’ve used up way more energy than that should require.\n\nAnd it’s frustrating because I keep thinking, “Okay, just rest, just sleep, you’ll reset,” but sleep doesn’t really fix it. I can do something small and then feel like I need to recover from it, which makes me feel kind of ridiculous, because these aren’t huge physical tasks or anything. It’s just this tiredness that lingers and gets worse when I try to push through.\n\nDoctor: When you wake up after a full night of sleep, do you usually feel refreshed?\n\nPatient: No, not really. That’s actually one of the more frustrating parts, because I’ll think, “Okay, I got a full night, so tomorrow should be better,” and then I wake up and it’s like… I don’t feel restored. I might technically have slept, but I still feel heavy and drained, almost like I’m starting the day already used up.\n\nAnd then even small things feel bigger than they should. Like unloading the dishwasher can feel like this whole task I have to gear myself up for, or reading for my certificate class starts out okay and then suddenly feels like too much. So, no, I wouldn’t say I usually wake up refreshed, even if I slept enough hours.\n\nDoctor: Has this exhaustion been going on for six months or longer?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s been six months. It feels long, honestly, because when you’re in it every day it kind of stretches out, but if I’m trying to be accurate, this quieter, low-energy stretch has been more like about three months.\n\nThat’s when I started noticing that I wasn’t just tired from a bad week or needing a weekend to catch up. It was more like my interest in things got lower and this heavy fatigue settled in and didn’t really lift the way I expected it to. So… not six months or longer, but definitely a few months. Maybe around three.\n\nDoctor: For the past few months, have you felt sad or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I’ve definitely felt… low, I guess, or kind of weighed down at times, but “sad most of the day nearly every day” feels harder for me to say yes to.\n\nIt’s not like I’m crying all day or constantly thinking, “I’m sad.” A lot of the time it’s more like I feel flat, or slowed down, or just not very bright inside, if that makes sense. But then there are also parts of the day where I’m distracted or just going through the motions, and I don’t know if that counts as feeling sad or not.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say it’s been clearly sad all day every day, but something has definitely felt off emotionally for the past few months.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few months, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think that’s probably the clearest part of it, honestly. I’ve sort of stopped caring about the things that usually make me feel like me.\n\nLike, I used to sketch pretty regularly—not necessarily anything amazing, just little studies or ideas in a notebook—and normally that feels grounding. But lately the sketchbook just sits there, and I’ll look at it and think I should want to pick it up, but I don’t. Same with going to gallery openings. That used to be something I’d actually get excited about, even just getting dressed and wandering around and seeing what people were making. Now it feels like this whole effort, and I can’t really imagine enjoying it once I’m there.\n\nCooking has been like that too. I used to like trying recipes or making something nice for myself, and now it’s more like… I don’t care enough. Yoga feels similar. And even tennis with a friend, which should be fun and social and active, just doesn’t pull me in the way it normally would.\n\nSo, yes. It’s not that I hate those things now, exactly. It’s more like the part of me that used to feel interested or pleased by them has gotten really quiet.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few months, has your appetite changed?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. Not in a dramatic way where I’m, like, deliberately not eating or anything, but my appetite has been pretty off.\n\nSome days I’ll get to later in the afternoon and realize I’ve basically only had coffee and maybe a few bites of something—like half a piece of toast or a couple forkfuls of leftovers—and I didn’t even really notice I was skipping meals. Food just doesn’t sound that appealing, or it feels like too much effort to decide what to eat.\n\nAnd I have noticed my jeans are looser. I wasn’t trying to lose weight, so that kind of stood out to me. It’s not something I was aiming for; it just sort of happened along with not eating very much some days.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I don’t think I’ve had clear thoughts like, “I’m going to hurt myself,” if that’s what you mean. And I don’t have, like, a plan or anything I’m sitting with.\n\nBut the “life isn’t worth living” part is harder, because sometimes when I’m really low or just worn out, I’ll have this kind of vague feeling of, “What is the point of doing all of this?” Not in a dramatic way, more like this heavy, exhausted thought that everything feels like too much. Then it passes, or I distract myself, or I just sort of keep going.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t been actively trying to hurt myself. But I have had those darker, pointless-feeling thoughts around the edges sometimes, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been moving or speaking more slowly than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not every single day, but on the slower days it’s pretty noticeable to me.\n\nIt’s like my body is moving through wet cement, if that makes sense. I’ll be doing something basic, like getting up from the couch or walking to the kitchen, and everything just feels heavier and slower than it should. Not painful, exactly, just like there’s this delay between deciding to move and actually moving.\n\nAnd talking can feel that way too. My spouse will ask me something simple, like what I want to do or whether I need anything, and I’ll just sit there for a weirdly long time trying to answer. I can tell it’s taking too long, which makes me feel awkward, but it’s like the words don’t come out right away. I’m not trying to ignore them. It’s more like everything in me is lagging.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually sensitive to cold lately?\n\nPatient: Um… unusually sensitive to cold? I don’t think so. I mean, I get cold sometimes, but not in a way that’s stood out to me as different or weird.\n\nLike, I’ll put on a sweatshirt if the house is chilly, but I haven’t been thinking, “Why am I freezing all the time?” or anything like that. I guess I haven’t really been tracking it closely, but no, nothing noticeable comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Before this started about three months ago, have you ever had another period lasting at least two weeks when you felt depressed or lost interest in things most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I’ve had low stretches before, like times where I felt really discouraged or kind of flat, but I don’t know if I could say it was a full two weeks most days, if that makes sense.\n\nI’m trying to think back, and it all gets a little blurry because I don’t usually mark it on a calendar or anything. I can remember bad days, or maybe a bad week here and there, but whether it was really depressed or not interested in things nearly every day for two weeks… I don’t know that I can confidently say yes.\n\nSo I guess my answer is maybe, but I’m not sure. Nothing as clear in my memory as, “that was definitely a two-week period.”\n\nDoctor: Before this current stretch, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I know you just asked that in a slightly different way, and I’m trying not to over-answer it, but… I really don’t know.\n\nBefore this current stretch, I’ve definitely had times where I felt down or kind of emotionally shut off, like I wasn’t as interested in things as I normally would be. But when you say nearly every day for at least two weeks, that’s the part I can’t honestly pin down. I can remember patches of feeling bad, but I don’t know if they lasted that long or if it was more like a few bad days mixed in with some okay days.\n\nI don’t want to say yes just because I’ve had rough periods, if that makes sense. This one feels more clear to me because it’s recent and I’ve been aware of it. The older stuff is just harder for me to sort out. So… maybe there were times like that, but I’m not confident enough to say there definitely were.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt unusually high, energized, or irritable for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. There have been times where for weeks I felt almost, I don’t know, lit up. Like everything suddenly seemed possible and I was completely sure things were about to be amazing, even if nothing had really changed enough to explain why I felt that way.\n\nAnd it wasn’t just being in a good mood for a day or two. It felt bigger than that, like I had this energy or excitement running through me. But at the same time, I could get really touchy. My husband would make some small comment about the budget, or something around the house, and I’d just snap at him like he had said something terrible. Then later I’d think, why did I react that strongly?\n\nSo yes, there have been periods like that, and they lasted more than several days. More like weeks, at least the ones I’m thinking of.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks when you felt lit up, did you need less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I’m trying to think about those weeks specifically, and I don’t have a clean memory of, like, “Oh yes, I was only sleeping three hours and felt fine,” or anything that clear.\n\nI do remember feeling more awake or keyed up in general during those times, but whether I actually needed less sleep than usual… I don’t know. I might have stayed up later some nights because I felt like my mind was still going, but I can’t confidently say my sleep was dramatically reduced or that I didn’t feel tired afterward.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is: maybe a little, but I didn’t track it, and it’s not one of the things I remember clearly.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks when you felt lit up, were you more driven than usual to start or do a lot of projects or activities?\n\nPatient: Yes. That part I remember pretty clearly, actually. I would suddenly feel like I had this whole plan for my life and I needed to act on all of it immediately.\n\nLike, I started contacting galleries, and I was drafting sections for a newsletter, and then I was planning these community workshop ideas, and at the same time trying to line up freelance clients. It wasn’t just one project where I was excited and focused — it was like five different directions at once, and they all felt urgent and totally realistic in the moment.\n\nLooking back, it was way more than I could actually follow through on. I’d start making lists and sending emails and sketching out plans, and I’d feel like, “Okay, this is it, I’m finally getting everything moving.” But then there was just too much happening, and a lot of it didn’t really get finished or went nowhere because I had taken on more than I could manage. So yes, definitely more driven than usual.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks when you felt lit up, did you feel more confident or powerful than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes, I did. I don’t think I would have called it “powerful” at the time, because that sounds so dramatic, but… I definitely felt unusually sure of myself. Like I had finally figured out something everyone else hadn’t, or like I was suddenly capable of making these huge things happen if I just pushed hard enough.\n\nI remember honestly believing I could take the newsletter idea, get a gallery partnership going, and turn that into some kind of consulting business or bigger professional thing almost overnight. And I mean, looking back, I hadn’t really worked out the basics. I didn’t have a clear business plan, or enough contacts, or even a realistic sense of how all of it would actually function. But at the time it didn’t feel unrealistic. It felt obvious, like, “Of course I can do this. Why wouldn’t this work?”\n\nIt wasn’t just normal confidence, I guess. It was more like this certainty that I was on the edge of something big and that I could pull it all together faster than anyone would expect. I felt kind of unstoppable in that way, even though now I can see there were a lot of missing pieces.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks when you felt lit up, did that cause serious problems in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it catastrophic, but it definitely caused problems. More than I wanted to admit at the time.\n\nThe biggest thing is that I couldn’t actually keep everything organized. I’d have all these plans and ideas and I’d be starting things, but the normal stuff in my life started slipping. I fell behind in my certificate coursework because I kept thinking I was going to catch up later, or I’d sit down to do it and get pulled into some other plan that felt more urgent. And then suddenly I was behind enough that it felt embarrassing to even look at it.\n\nAt home, things piled up too. Bills, meals, basic household tasks — not because I didn’t care, but because I was just not following through consistently. I’d have these bursts where I thought I was going to reorganize everything, and then somehow the actual practical things didn’t get done. My spouse had to step in more, and I feel bad about that because it wasn’t fair. It put more on them.\n\nAnd socially, I kind of pulled back from people and from art commitments I normally really care about. Which is strange, because I felt like I was doing all these big important things, but in reality I was dropping the regular things and relationships that keep me steady.\n\nSo yes, it did cause problems. I guess “serious” depends how you define it, but it affected my schoolwork, my home life, and my relationships enough that I can’t really say it was just harmless energy.\n\nDoctor: Did any of those unusually energized or lit-up periods last at least one full week?\n\nPatient: Yes. It wasn’t just a day or two where I was in a good mood or had a burst of motivation. It lasted for weeks at a time, that kind of lit-up feeling where everything seemed exciting and possible and like it was all about to turn into something amazing.\n\nI remember being really sure of myself during those stretches, like things were finally clicking and I just had to keep moving with it. But it wasn’t only upbeat, exactly. I could get irritable too, especially at home. If my husband made a small comment about the budget or the house, things that were probably reasonable to bring up, I’d snap at him like he was trying to slow me down or criticize me. At the time it felt like he was being negative, but looking back, my reaction was bigger than the comment deserved.\n\nSo yes, definitely at least a full week. More like several weeks, from what I remember.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. More like several weeks, from what I remember.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring those lit-up periods, did you ever have beliefs that other people said were clearly untrue or unrealistic?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 33 / no_occupation / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Inflated_Self_Esteem\nmanifestation: \"I honestly believed I could turn the newsletter, a gallery partnership, and a consulting business into something big almost overnight, even though I hadn’t really worked out the basics.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. Not like I believed something completely impossible in a bizarre way, but I definitely believed things that other people thought were unrealistic. At the time, I didn’t experience them as unrealistic at all.

The main one was this whole idea that I could take the newsletter I was working on, connect it with a gallery partnership, and somehow turn that into a consulting business or this much bigger professional thing almost immediately. Like, almost overnight, in my mind. I was talking about it as if it was just a matter of pushing hard enough and making the right moves, and then it would all fall into place.

And people around me were kind of trying to point out that I hadn’t really figured out the basics. I didn’t have a real plan, or enough contacts, or a clear sense of how the money would work, or what I was actually offering. But when they said that, I remember feeling like they just didn’t understand the scale of it yet. Like they were being too cautious or small-minded.

Looking back, it feels embarrassing, because there were so many steps missing. But at the time, I honestly believed I could make it into something big very quickly, and I was really certain about it.

But lately the sketchbook just sits there, and I’ll look at it and think I should want to pick it up, but I don’t. Same with going to gallery openings. That used to be something I’d actually get excited about, even just getting dressed and wandering around and seeing what people were making. Now it feels like this whole effort, and I can’t really imagine enjoying it once I’m there.\n\nCooking has been like that too. I used to like trying recipes or making something nice for myself, and now it’s more like… I don’t care enough. Yoga feels similar. And even tennis with a friend, which should be fun and social and active, just doesn’t pull me in the way it normally would.\n\nSo, yes. It’s not that I hate those things now, exactly. It’s more like the part of me that used to feel interested or pleased by them has gotten really quiet.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few months, has your appetite changed?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. Not in a dramatic way where I’m, like, deliberately not eating or anything, but my appetite has been pretty off.\n\nSome days I’ll get to later in the afternoon and realize I’ve basically only had coffee and maybe a few bites of something—like half a piece of toast or a couple forkfuls of leftovers—and I didn’t even really notice I was skipping meals. Food just doesn’t sound that appealing, or it feels like too much effort to decide what to eat.\n\nAnd I have noticed my jeans are looser. I wasn’t trying to lose weight, so that kind of stood out to me. It’s not something I was aiming for; it just sort of happened along with not eating very much some days.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I don’t think I’ve had clear thoughts like, “I’m going to hurt myself,” if that’s what you mean. And I don’t have, like, a plan or anything I’m sitting with.\n\nBut the “life isn’t worth living” part is harder, because sometimes when I’m really low or just worn out, I’ll have this kind of vague feeling of, “What is the point of doing all of this?” Not in a dramatic way, more like this heavy, exhausted thought that everything feels like too much. Then it passes, or I distract myself, or I just sort of keep going.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t been actively trying to hurt myself. But I have had those darker, pointless-feeling thoughts around the edges sometimes, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been moving or speaking more slowly than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not every single day, but on the slower days it’s pretty noticeable to me.\n\nIt’s like my body is moving through wet cement, if that makes sense. I’ll be doing something basic, like getting up from the couch or walking to the kitchen, and everything just feels heavier and slower than it should. Not painful, exactly, just like there’s this delay between deciding to move and actually moving.\n\nAnd talking can feel that way too. My spouse will ask me something simple, like what I want to do or whether I need anything, and I’ll just sit there for a weirdly long time trying to answer. I can tell it’s taking too long, which makes me feel awkward, but it’s like the words don’t come out right away. I’m not trying to ignore them. It’s more like everything in me is lagging.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually sensitive to cold lately?\n\nPatient: Um… unusually sensitive to cold? I don’t think so. I mean, I get cold sometimes, but not in a way that’s stood out to me as different or weird.\n\nLike, I’ll put on a sweatshirt if the house is chilly, but I haven’t been thinking, “Why am I freezing all the time?” or anything like that. I guess I haven’t really been tracking it closely, but no, nothing noticeable comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Before this started about three months ago, have you ever had another period lasting at least two weeks when you felt depressed or lost interest in things most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I’ve had low stretches before, like times where I felt really discouraged or kind of flat, but I don’t know if I could say it was a full two weeks most days, if that makes sense.\n\nI’m trying to think back, and it all gets a little blurry because I don’t usually mark it on a calendar or anything. I can remember bad days, or maybe a bad week here and there, but whether it was really depressed or not interested in things nearly every day for two weeks… I don’t know that I can confidently say yes.\n\nSo I guess my answer is maybe, but I’m not sure. Nothing as clear in my memory as, “that was definitely a two-week period.”\n\nDoctor: Before this current stretch, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I know you just asked that in a slightly different way, and I’m trying not to over-answer it, but… I really don’t know.\n\nBefore this current stretch, I’ve definitely had times where I felt down or kind of emotionally shut off, like I wasn’t as interested in things as I normally would be. But when you say nearly every day for at least two weeks, that’s the part I can’t honestly pin down. I can remember patches of feeling bad, but I don’t know if they lasted that long or if it was more like a few bad days mixed in with some okay days.\n\nI don’t want to say yes just because I’ve had rough periods, if that makes sense. This one feels more clear to me because it’s recent and I’ve been aware of it. The older stuff is just harder for me to sort out. So… maybe there were times like that, but I’m not confident enough to say there definitely were.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt unusually high, energized, or irritable for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. There have been times where for weeks I felt almost, I don’t know, lit up. Like everything suddenly seemed possible and I was completely sure things were about to be amazing, even if nothing had really changed enough to explain why I felt that way.\n\nAnd it wasn’t just being in a good mood for a day or two. It felt bigger than that, like I had this energy or excitement running through me. But at the same time, I could get really touchy. My husband would make some small comment about the budget, or something around the house, and I’d just snap at him like he had said something terrible. Then later I’d think, why did I react that strongly?\n\nSo yes, there have been periods like that, and they lasted more than several days. More like weeks, at least the ones I’m thinking of.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks when you felt lit up, did you need less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I’m trying to think about those weeks specifically, and I don’t have a clean memory of, like, “Oh yes, I was only sleeping three hours and felt fine,” or anything that clear.\n\nI do remember feeling more awake or keyed up in general during those times, but whether I actually needed less sleep than usual… I don’t know. I might have stayed up later some nights because I felt like my mind was still going, but I can’t confidently say my sleep was dramatically reduced or that I didn’t feel tired afterward.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is: maybe a little, but I didn’t track it, and it’s not one of the things I remember clearly.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks when you felt lit up, were you more driven than usual to start or do a lot of projects or activities?\n\nPatient: Yes. That part I remember pretty clearly, actually. I would suddenly feel like I had this whole plan for my life and I needed to act on all of it immediately.\n\nLike, I started contacting galleries, and I was drafting sections for a newsletter, and then I was planning these community workshop ideas, and at the same time trying to line up freelance clients. It wasn’t just one project where I was excited and focused — it was like five different directions at once, and they all felt urgent and totally realistic in the moment.\n\nLooking back, it was way more than I could actually follow through on. I’d start making lists and sending emails and sketching out plans, and I’d feel like, “Okay, this is it, I’m finally getting everything moving.” But then there was just too much happening, and a lot of it didn’t really get finished or went nowhere because I had taken on more than I could manage. So yes, definitely more driven than usual.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks when you felt lit up, did you feel more confident or powerful than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes, I did. I don’t think I would have called it “powerful” at the time, because that sounds so dramatic, but… I definitely felt unusually sure of myself. Like I had finally figured out something everyone else hadn’t, or like I was suddenly capable of making these huge things happen if I just pushed hard enough.\n\nI remember honestly believing I could take the newsletter idea, get a gallery partnership going, and turn that into some kind of consulting business or bigger professional thing almost overnight. And I mean, looking back, I hadn’t really worked out the basics. I didn’t have a clear business plan, or enough contacts, or even a realistic sense of how all of it would actually function. But at the time it didn’t feel unrealistic. It felt obvious, like, “Of course I can do this. Why wouldn’t this work?”\n\nIt wasn’t just normal confidence, I guess. It was more like this certainty that I was on the edge of something big and that I could pull it all together faster than anyone would expect. I felt kind of unstoppable in that way, even though now I can see there were a lot of missing pieces.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks when you felt lit up, did that cause serious problems in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it catastrophic, but it definitely caused problems. More than I wanted to admit at the time.\n\nThe biggest thing is that I couldn’t actually keep everything organized. I’d have all these plans and ideas and I’d be starting things, but the normal stuff in my life started slipping. I fell behind in my certificate coursework because I kept thinking I was going to catch up later, or I’d sit down to do it and get pulled into some other plan that felt more urgent. And then suddenly I was behind enough that it felt embarrassing to even look at it.\n\nAt home, things piled up too. Bills, meals, basic household tasks — not because I didn’t care, but because I was just not following through consistently. I’d have these bursts where I thought I was going to reorganize everything, and then somehow the actual practical things didn’t get done. My spouse had to step in more, and I feel bad about that because it wasn’t fair. It put more on them.\n\nAnd socially, I kind of pulled back from people and from art commitments I normally really care about. Which is strange, because I felt like I was doing all these big important things, but in reality I was dropping the regular things and relationships that keep me steady.\n\nSo yes, it did cause problems. I guess “serious” depends how you define it, but it affected my schoolwork, my home life, and my relationships enough that I can’t really say it was just harmless energy.\n\nDoctor: Did any of those unusually energized or lit-up periods last at least one full week?\n\nPatient: Yes. It wasn’t just a day or two where I was in a good mood or had a burst of motivation. It lasted for weeks at a time, that kind of lit-up feeling where everything seemed exciting and possible and like it was all about to turn into something amazing.\n\nI remember being really sure of myself during those stretches, like things were finally clicking and I just had to keep moving with it. But it wasn’t only upbeat, exactly. I could get irritable too, especially at home. If my husband made a small comment about the budget or the house, things that were probably reasonable to bring up, I’d snap at him like he was trying to slow me down or criticize me. At the time it felt like he was being negative, but looking back, my reaction was bigger than the comment deserved.\n\nSo yes, definitely at least a full week. More like several weeks, from what I remember.\n\nDoctor: During those lit-up periods, did you ever have beliefs that other people said were clearly untrue or unrealistic?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not like I believed something completely impossible in a bizarre way, but I definitely believed things that other people thought were unrealistic. At the time, I didn’t experience them as unrealistic at all.\n\nThe main one was this whole idea that I could take the newsletter I was working on, connect it with a gallery partnership, and somehow turn that into a consulting business or this much bigger professional thing almost immediately. Like, almost overnight, in my mind. I was talking about it as if it was just a matter of pushing hard enough and making the right moves, and then it would all fall into place.\n\nAnd people around me were kind of trying to point out that I hadn’t really figured out the basics. I didn’t have a real plan, or enough contacts, or a clear sense of how the money would work, or what I was actually offering. But when they said that, I remember feeling like they just didn’t understand the scale of it yet. Like they were being too cautious or small-minded.\n\nLooking back, it feels embarrassing, because there were so many steps missing. But at the time, I honestly believed I could make it into something big very quickly, and I was really certain about it."
